The Wikipedia article about *[Virgin Killer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Killer)*—a 1976 album from the German rock band [Scorpions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scorpions_\(band\))—features a picture of the album's original cover, which depicts a naked [prepubescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preadolescence#Prepubescence,_puberty,_and_age_range) girl. The original release cover caused controversy and was replaced in some countries. In December 2008, access to the Wikipedia article *Virgin Killer* was blocked for four days by most [Internet service providers](100245.md) in the United Kingdom after the [Internet Watch Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_Watch_Foundation) (IWF) decided the album cover was a potentially illegal indecent image and added the article's URL to a "blacklist" it supplies to British internet service providers.[^cite_note-Register_ISP_censorship-288]

In April 2010, Sanger wrote a letter to the Federal Bureau of Investigation, outlining his concerns that two categories of images on [Wikimedia Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikimedia_Commons) contained child pornography, and were in violation of [US federal obscenity law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_obscenity_law).[^cite_note-289][^cite_note-Inquirer_child_abuse_allegations-290] Sanger later clarified that the images, which were related to [pedophilia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedophilia) and one about [lolicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lolicon), were not of real children, but said that they constituted "obscene visual representations of the sexual abuse of children", under the [PROTECT Act of 2003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_pornography_laws_in_the_United_States#1466A_%E2%80%93_Obscene_visual_representations_of_the_sexual_abuse_of_children).[^cite_note-The_Register-April-291] That law bans photographic child pornography and cartoon images and drawings of children that are [obscene under American law](22036467.md#United_States_obscenity_law).[^cite_note-The_Register-April-291] Sanger also expressed concerns about access to the images on Wikipedia in schools.[^cite_note-TET_child_porn_accusations-292]

[Wikimedia Foundation](18618509.md) spokesman Jay Walsh strongly rejected Sanger's accusation,[^cite_note-AFP-293] saying that Wikipedia did not have "material we would deem to be illegal. If we did, we would remove it."[^cite_note-AFP-293] Following the complaint by Sanger, Wales deleted sexual images without consulting the community. After volunteer editors argued that the decision was made hastily, Wales voluntarily gave up some powers he had held up as part of his co-founder status. He wrote in a message to the Wikimedia Foundation mailing-list that this action was "in the interest of encouraging this discussion to be about real philosophical/content issues, rather than be about me and how quickly I acted".[^cite_note-BBC_News_Wales_cedes_rights-294] Critics, including [Wikipediocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipediocracy), noticed that many of the pornographic images deleted from Wikipedia since 2010 have reappeared.[^cite_note-XBIZ-295]

### Privacy

One [privacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privacy) concern in the case of Wikipedia regards one's right to remain a [private citizen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_citizen) rather than a [public figure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_figure) in the eyes of the law.[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297] It is a battle between the right to be anonymous in [cyberspace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyberspace) and the right to be anonymous in [real life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_life). The Wikimedia Foundation's [privacy policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privacy_policy) states, "we believe that you shouldn't have to provide personal information to participate in the free knowledge movement", and states that "personal information" may be shared "For legal reasons", "To Protect You, Ourselves & Others", or "To Understand & Experiment".[^cite_note-298]

In January 2006, a German court ordered the [German Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Wikipedia) shut down within Germany because it stated the full name of [Boris Floricic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tron_\(hacker\)), aka "Tron", a deceased hacker. On February 9, 2006, the injunction against Wikimedia Deutschland was overturned, with the court rejecting the notion that Tron's [right to privacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_privacy) or that of his parents was being violated.[^cite_note-heise_Tron_public_issue_1-299]

Wikipedia has a "Volunteer Response Team" that uses Znuny, a [free and open-source software](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_and_open-source_software) fork of [OTRS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OTRS)[^cite_note-300] to handle queries without having to reveal the identities of the involved parties. This is used, for example, in confirming the permission for using individual images and other media in the project.[^cite_note-301]

In late April 2023, the Wikimedia Foundation announced that Wikipedia will not submit to any age verifications that may be required by the UK's [Online Safety Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Online_Safety_Act_2023) legislation. Rebecca MacKinnon of the Wikimedia Foundation said that such checks would run counter to the website's commitment to minimal data collection on its contributors and readers.[^cite_note-302]

### Sexism

Main article: [Gender bias on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_bias_on_Wikipedia)

Wikipedia was described in 2015 as harboring a battleground culture of [sexism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexism) and [harassment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harassment).[^cite_note-Paling-303][^cite_note-304] The perceived tolerance of abusive language was a reason put forth in 2013 for the gender gap in Wikipedia editorship.[^cite_note-305] [Edit-a-thons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edit-a-thon) have been held to encourage female editors and increase the coverage of women's topics.[^cite_note-306]

In May 2018, a Wikipedia editor rejected a submitted article about [Donna Strickland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donna_Strickland) due to lack of coverage in the media.[^cite_note-307][^cite_note-Schlanger-2018-308] Five months later, Strickland won a [Nobel Prize in Physics](52497.md) "for groundbreaking inventions in the field of laser physics", becoming the third woman to ever receive the award.[^cite_note-Schlanger-2018-308][^cite_note-309] Prior to winning the award, Strickland's only mention on Wikipedia was in the article about her collaborator and co-winner of the award [Gérard Mourou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%A9rard_Mourou).[^cite_note-Schlanger-2018-308] Her exclusion from Wikipedia led to accusations of sexism, but Corinne Purtill writing for *[Quartz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quartz_\(publication\))* argued that "it's also a pointed lesson in the hazards of gender bias in media, and of the broader consequences of underrepresentation."[^cite_note-Purtill-2018-310] Purtill attributes the issue to the gender bias in media coverage.[^cite_note-Purtill-2018-310]

A comprehensive 2008 survey, published in 2016, by Julia B. Bear of [Stony Brook University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stony_Brook_University)'s College of Business and Benjamin Collier of [Carnegie Mellon University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnegie_Mellon_University) found significant gender differences in confidence in expertise, discomfort with editing, and response to critical feedback. "Women reported less confidence in their expertise, expressed greater discomfort with editing (which typically involves conflict), and reported more negative responses to critical feedback compared to men."[^cite_note-311]

### *Nine Theses*

*"Nine Theses"* refers to a reform proposal published by Larry Sanger in October 2025. Sanger's *Nine Theses* on Wikipedia presents a critical assessment and reform agenda for Wikipedia. The proposal is part of his broader effort to address what Sanger perceives as systemic issues within Wikipedia, which include, ideological bias, lack of transparency in the editor hierarchies, and an ineffective consensus-based decision-making procedure.[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313]

## Operation

### Wikimedia Foundation and affiliate movements

Main article: [Wikimedia Foundation](18618509.md)



Sue Gardner, first executive director, in 2013 at [Wikimania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikimania)

Wikipedia is hosted and funded by the [Wikimedia Foundation](18618509.md), a non-profit organization which also operates Wikipedia-related projects such as [Wiktionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wiktionary) and [Wikibooks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikibooks).[^cite_note-314] The foundation relies on public contributions and grants to fund its mission.[^cite_note-315][^cite_note-financialstatements-316] The foundation's 2020 Internal Revenue Service [Form 990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Form_990) shows revenue of \$124.6 million and expenses of almost \$112.2 million, with assets of about \$191.2 million and liabilities of almost \$11 million.[^cite_note-317]

In May 2014, Wikimedia Foundation named [Lila Tretikov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lila_Tretikov) as its second executive director, taking over for Sue Gardner.[^cite_note-318] *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* reported on May 1, 2014, that Tretikov's information technology background, from her years at University of California offers Wikipedia an opportunity to develop in more concentrated directions guided by her often repeated position statement that, "Information, like air, wants to be free."[^cite_note-Jeff_Elder_2014-319][^cite_note-nytimes.com-320] The same *Wall Street Journal* article reported these directions of development according to an interview with spokesman Jay Walsh of Wikimedia, who "said Tretikov would address that issue ([paid advocacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conflict-of-interest_editing_on_Wikipedia)) as a priority. 'We are really pushing toward more transparency ... We are reinforcing that paid advocacy is not welcome.' Initiatives to involve greater diversity of contributors, better mobile support of Wikipedia, new geo-location tools to find local content more easily, and more tools for users in the second and third world are also priorities", Walsh said.[^cite_note-Jeff_Elder_2014-319]

Following the departure of Tretikov from Wikipedia due to issues concerning the use of the "superprotection" feature which some language versions of Wikipedia have adopted,[^cite_note-321] [Katherine Maher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katherine_Maher) became the third executive director of the Wikimedia Foundation in June 2016.[^cite_note-322] Maher stated that one of her priorities would be the issue of editor harassment endemic to Wikipedia as identified by the Wikipedia board in December. She said to *[Bloomberg Businessweek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloomberg_Businessweek)* regarding the harassment issue that: "It establishes a sense within the community that this is a priority ... \[and that correction requires that] it has to be more than words."[^cite_note-Bloomberg_2016-188]

Maher served as executive director until April 2021.[^cite_note-axios-323] [Maryana Iskander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryana_Iskander) was named the incoming CEO in September 2021, and took over that role in January 2022. She stated that one of her focuses would be increasing diversity in the Wikimedia community.[^cite_note-324] Iskander served until January 2026, when [Bernadette Meehan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernadette_Meehan) took over as CEO.

Wikipedia is also supported by many organizations and groups that are affiliated with the Wikimedia Foundation but independently run, called [Wikimedia movement affiliates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikimedia_movement_affiliates). These include [Wikimedia chapters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Wikimedia_chapters) (which are national or sub-national organizations, such as Wikimedia Deutschland and Wikimedia France), thematic organizations (such as Amical Wikimedia for the [Catalan language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_language) community), and user groups. These affiliates participate in the promotion, development, and funding of Wikipedia.[^cite_note-325]

### Software operations and support

See also: [MediaWiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MediaWiki)

The operation of Wikipedia depends on [MediaWiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MediaWiki), a custom-made, [free](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_software) and open source [wiki software](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wiki_software) platform written in [PHP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PHP) and built upon the [MySQL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MySQL) database system.[^cite_note-nedworks_database_system-326] The software incorporates programming features such as a [macro language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macro_\(computer_science\)), [variables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variable_\(programming\)), a [transclusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transclusion) system for [templates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Web_template_system), and [URL redirection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/URL_redirection).[^cite_note-327] MediaWiki is licensed under the [GNU General Public License](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GNU_General_Public_License) (GPL) and it is used by all Wikimedia projects, as well as many other wiki projects.[^cite_note-nedworks_database_system-326][^cite_note-328] Originally, Wikipedia ran on [UseModWiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UseModWiki) written in [Perl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perl) by Clifford Adams (Phase I), which initially required [CamelCase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camel_case) for article hyperlinks; the present double bracket style was incorporated later.[^cite_note-329] Starting in January 2002 (Phase II), Wikipedia began running on a [PHP wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PhpWiki) engine with a MySQL database; this software was custom-made for Wikipedia by [Magnus Manske](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnus_Manske). The Phase II software was repeatedly modified to accommodate the [exponentially increasing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exponential_growth) demand. In July 2002 (Phase III), Wikipedia shifted to the third-generation software, MediaWiki, originally written by [Lee Daniel Crocker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Daniel_Crocker).

Several MediaWiki extensions are installed to extend the functionality of the MediaWiki software.[^cite_note-WP_extensions_installed-330] In April 2005, a [Lucene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apache_Lucene) extension[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332] was added to MediaWiki's built-in search and Wikipedia switched from MySQL to Lucene for searching. Lucene was later replaced by CirrusSearch which is based on [Elasticsearch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elasticsearch).[^cite_note-333] In July 2013, after extensive beta testing, a [WYSIWYG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WYSIWYG) (What You See Is What You Get) extension, [VisualEditor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VisualEditor), was opened to public use.[^cite_note-thenextwebve-334][^cite_note-335][^cite_note-TheEconomistVE-336] It was met with much rejection and criticism, and was described as "slow and buggy".[^cite_note-Orlowski,_Andrew-337] The feature was changed from opt-out to opt-in afterward.[^cite_note-338]

### Automated editing

Main article: [Wikipedia bots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia_bots)

Computer programs called [bots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_bot) have often been used to perform simple and repetitive tasks, such as correcting common misspellings and stylistic issues, or to start articles such as geography entries in a standard format from statistical data.[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-meetbots-340][^cite_note-341] One controversial contributor, [Sverker Johansson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sverker_Johansson), created articles with his bot [Lsjbot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lsjbot), which was reported to create up to 10,000 articles on the Swedish Wikipedia on certain days.[^cite_note-342] Additionally, there are bots designed to automatically notify editors when they make common editing errors (such as unmatched quotes or unmatched parentheses).[^cite_note-343] Edits falsely identified by bots as the work of a banned editor can be restored by other editors. An anti-vandal bot is programmed to detect and revert vandalism quickly.[^cite_note-meetbots-340] Bots are able to indicate edits from particular accounts or [IP address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IP_address) ranges, as occurred at the time of the shooting down of the [MH17 jet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia_Airlines_Flight_17) in July 2014 when it was reported that edits were made via IPs controlled by the Russian government.[^cite_note-344] Bots on Wikipedia must be approved before activation.[^cite_note-345] According to [Andrew Lih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Lih), the current expansion of Wikipedia to millions of articles would be difficult to envision without the use of such bots.[^cite_note-346]

In March 2026, an [AI agent](78823217.md), Tom, edited Wikipedia under the account TomWikiAssist. The account was blocked for being an unapproved bot.[^cite_note-347]

### Hardware operations and support

Diagram showing flow of data between Wikipedia's servers.

Overview of system architecture as of August 2022

As of 2021, page requests are first passed to a front-end layer of [Varnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varnish_\(software\)) caching servers and back-end layer [caching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caching) is done by [Apache Traffic Server](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apache_Traffic_Server).[^cite_note-\:14-348] Requests that cannot be served from the Varnish cache are sent to load-balancing servers running the [Linux Virtual Server](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linux_Virtual_Server) software, which in turn pass them to one of the Apache web servers for page rendering from the database.[^cite_note-\:14-348] The web servers deliver pages as requested, performing page rendering for all the language editions of Wikipedia. To increase speed further, rendered pages are cached in a distributed memory cache until invalidated, allowing page rendering to be skipped entirely for the most common page accesses.[^cite_note-349]

Wikipedia currently runs on dedicated [clusters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_cluster) of [Linux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linux) servers running the [Debian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debian) operating system.[^cite_note-350] By January 22, 2013, Wikipedia had migrated its primary data center to an [Equinix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equinix) facility in [Ashburn, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashburn,_Virginia).[^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352] A second application data center was created in 2014 in [Carrollton, Texas](151136.md), to improve Wikipedia's reliability.[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354] Both datacenters work as the primary one, in alternate semesters, with the other one working as secondary datacenter.[^cite_note-355] In 2017, Wikipedia installed a caching cluster in an Equinix facility in [Singapore](27318.md), the first of its kind in Asia.[^cite_note-356] In 2022, a caching data center was opened in [Marseille](40888948.md), France.[^cite_note-357] In 2024, a caching data center was opened in [São Paulo](390875.md), the first of its kind in South America.[^cite_note-Magru-358] As of November 2024, caching clusters are located in [Amsterdam](844.md), San Francisco, Singapore, Marseille, and São Paulo.[^cite_note-\:13-359][^cite_note-360]

### Internal research and operational development

Following growing amounts of incoming donations in 2013 exceeding seven digits,[^cite_note-Simonite-2013-54] the Foundation has reached a threshold of assets which qualify its consideration under the principles of [industrial organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_organization) economics to indicate the need for the re-investment of donations into the internal research and development of the Foundation.[^cite_note-361] Two projects of such internal research and development have been the creation of a Visual Editor and the "Thank" tab in the edit history, which were developed to improve issues of editor attrition.[^cite_note-Simonite-2013-54][^cite_note-Orlowski,_Andrew-337] The estimates for reinvestment by industrial organizations into internal research and development was studied by [Adam Jaffe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_B._Jaffe), who recorded that the range of 4% to 25% annually was to be recommended, with high-end technology requiring the higher level of support for internal reinvestment.[^cite_note-Trajtenberg-2002-362] At the 2013 level of contributions for Wikimedia presently documented as 45 million dollars,[^cite_note-363] the computed budget level recommended by Jaffe for reinvestment into internal research and development is between 1.8 million and 11.3 million dollars annually.[^cite_note-Trajtenberg-2002-362] In 2019, the level of contributions were reported by the Wikimedia Foundation as being at \$120 million annually,[^cite_note-364] updating the Jaffe estimates for the higher level of support to between \$3.08 million and \$19.2 million annually.[^cite_note-Trajtenberg-2002-362]

### Internal news publications

Main article: [*The Signpost*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Signpost)

Multiple Wikimedia projects have internal news publications. [Wikimedia](18618509.md)'s online newspaper *[The Signpost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Signpost)* was founded in 2005 by Michael Snow, a Wikipedia administrator who would join the Wikimedia Foundation's board of trustees in 2008.[^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366] The publication covers news and events from the English Wikipedia, the Wikimedia Foundation, and [Wikipedia's sister projects](18618509.md#Projects_and_initiatives).[^cite_note-367]

### The Wikipedia Library

For information for Wikipedia editors, see [Wikipedia\:The Wikipedia Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:The_Wikipedia_Library).

Wikipedia Library logo

Logo of the Wikipedia Library

Wikipedia editors sometimes struggle to access paywalled sources needed to improve a subject.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcDowellVetter202264-368] The Wikipedia Library is a resource for Wikipedia editors which provides free access to a wide range of [digital publications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_publishing), so that they can consult and cite these while editing the encyclopedia.[^cite_note-orlowitz-369][^cite_note-bna-370] Over 60 publishers have partnered with The Wikipedia Library to provide access to their resources: when [ICE Publishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICE_Publishing) joined in 2020, a spokesman said "By enabling free access to our content for Wikipedia editors, we hope to further the research community's resources – creating and updating Wikipedia entries on civil engineering which are read by thousands of monthly readers."[^cite_note-hall-371]

## Access to content

"Accessing Wikipedia" redirects here. For our accessibility guidelines, see [Wikipedia\:Manual of Style/Accessibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Manual_of_Style/Accessibility).

### Content licensing

When the project was started in 2001, all text in Wikipedia was covered by the [GNU Free Documentation License](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GNU_Free_Documentation_License) (GFDL), a [copyleft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copyleft) license permitting the redistribution, creation of derivative works, and commercial use of content while authors retain copyright of their work.[^cite_note-Wikipedia\:Copyrights-372] The GFDL was created for software manuals that come with [free software](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_software) programs licensed under the [GPL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GNU_General_Public_License). This made it a poor choice for a general reference work: for example, the GFDL requires the reprints of materials from Wikipedia to come with a full copy of the GFDL text.[^cite_note-373] In December 2002, the [Creative Commons license](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creative_Commons_license) was released; it was specifically designed for creative works in general, not just for software manuals. The Wikipedia project sought the switch to the Creative Commons.[^cite_note-WPF_switch_to_CC-374] Because the GFDL and Creative Commons were incompatible, in November 2008, following the request of the project, the [Free Software Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Software_Foundation) (FSF) released a new version of the GFDL designed specifically to allow Wikipedia to relicense its content to CC BY-SA by August 1, 2009.[^cite_note-375] In April 2009, Wikipedia and its sister projects held a community-wide referendum, which decided the switch in June 2009.[^cite_note-voteresult-376][^cite_note-MW_licensing_QA-377][^cite_note-MW_licensing_timeline_1-378][^cite_note-WP_blog_license_migration-379]

The handling of media files (e.g., image files) varies across language editions. Some language editions, such as the English Wikipedia, include non-free image files under [fair use](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_use) doctrine,[^cite_note-380] while the others have opted not to, in part because of the lack of fair use doctrines in their home countries (e.g. in [Japanese copyright law](18935247.md)). Media files covered by [free content](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_content) licenses (e.g. [Creative Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creative_Commons)' [CC BY-SA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CC_BY-SA)) are shared across language editions via [Wikimedia Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikimedia_Commons) repository, a project operated by the Wikimedia Foundation.[^cite_note-381] Wikipedia's accommodation of varying international copyright laws regarding images has led some to observe that its photographic coverage of topics lags behind the quality of the encyclopedic text.[^cite_note-NYT_photos_on_WP-382] The Wikimedia Foundation is not a licensor of content on Wikipedia or its related projects but merely a hosting service for contributors to and licensors of Wikipedia, a position which was successfully defended in 2004 in a court in France.[^cite_note-reuters_French_defamation_case-383][^cite_note-ars_tech_WP_dumb_suing_case-384]

### Methods of access

Since Wikipedia content is distributed under an open license, anyone can reuse or redistribute it at no charge.[^cite_note-385] The content of Wikipedia has been published in many forms, both online and offline, outside the Wikipedia website.

Thousands of "[mirror sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirror_site)" exist that republish content from Wikipedia; two prominent ones that also include content from other reference sources are [Reference.com](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reference.com) and [Answers.com](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Answers.com).[^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387] Another example is [Wapedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wapedia), which began to display Wikipedia content in a mobile-device-friendly format before Wikipedia itself did.[^cite_note-\:16-388] Some web [search engines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Search_engine) make special use of Wikipedia content when displaying search results: examples include [Microsoft Bing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microsoft_Bing) (via technology gained from [Powerset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Powerset_\(company\)))[^cite_note-bing_WP_research_and_referencing-389] and [DuckDuckGo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DuckDuckGo).

Collections of Wikipedia articles have been published on [optical discs](41458.md). An English version released in 2006 contained about 2,000 articles.[^cite_note-390] The Polish-language version from 2006 contains nearly 240,000 articles,[^cite_note-WM_polish_WP_on_dvd-391] the German-language version from 2007/2008 contains over 620,000 articles,[^cite_note-392] and the Spanish-language version from 2011 contains 886,000 articles.[^cite_note-393] Additionally, "Wikipedia for Schools", the Wikipedia series of CDs / DVDs produced by Wikipedia and [SOS Children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SOS_Children's_Villages_UK), is a free selection from Wikipedia designed for education towards children aged eight to seventeen.[^cite_note-394]

There have been efforts to put a select subset of Wikipedia's articles into printed book form.[^cite_note-WP_into_books_1-395][^cite_note-WP_schools_selection_1-396] Since 2009, tens of thousands of [print-on-demand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Print_on_demand) books that reproduced English, German, Russian, and French Wikipedia articles have been produced by the American company [Books LLC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Books_LLC) and by three Mauritian subsidiaries of the German publisher [VDM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VDM_Publishing).[^cite_note-FAZ-397]

The website [DBpedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DBpedia), begun in 2007, extracts data from the infoboxes and category declarations of the English-language Wikipedia.[^cite_note-398] Wikimedia has created the [Wikidata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikidata) project with a similar objective of storing the basic facts from each page of Wikipedia and other Wikimedia Foundation projects and make it available in a queryable [semantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semantic_Web) format, [RDF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resource_Description_Framework).[^cite_note-399] As of February 2023, it has over 101 million items.[^cite_note-400] [WikiReader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WikiReader) is a dedicated reader device that contains an offline copy of Wikipedia, which was launched by [OpenMoko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Openmoko) and first released in 2009.[^cite_note-401]

Obtaining the full contents of Wikipedia for reuse presents challenges, since direct cloning via a [web crawler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Web_crawler) is discouraged.[^cite_note-WP_DB_usage_policy_1-402] Wikipedia publishes "[dumps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Database_download)" of its contents, but these are text-only; as of 2023, there is no dump available of Wikipedia's images.[^cite_note-WP_image_data_dumps_1-403] [Wikimedia Enterprise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikimedia_Enterprise) is a for-profit solution to this.[^cite_note-404]

Several languages of Wikipedia also maintain a reference desk, where volunteers answer questions from the general public. According to a study by Pnina Shachaf in the *[Journal of Documentation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_Documentation)*, the quality of the Wikipedia reference desk is comparable to a standard library [reference desk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reference_desk), with an accuracy of 55 percent.[^cite_note-405]

#### Mobile access

See also: [List of Wikipedia mobile applications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Wikipedia_mobile_applications) and [Help\:Mobile access](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Mobile_access)



A mobile version showing the English Wikipedia's Main Page on October 2, 2024



Wikipedia page views by platform

Wikipedia's original medium was for users to read and edit content using any standard [web browser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Web_browser) through a fixed [Internet connection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_access). Although Wikipedia content has been accessible through the [mobile web](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_web) since July 2013, *The New York Times* on February 9, 2014, quoted [Erik Möller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erik_M%C3%B6ller), deputy director of the Wikimedia Foundation, stating that the transition of internet traffic from desktops to mobile devices was significant and a cause for concern and worry. The article in *The New York Times* reported the comparison statistics for mobile edits stating that, "Only 20 percent of the readership of the English-language Wikipedia comes via mobile devices, a figure substantially lower than the percentage of mobile traffic for other media sites, many of which approach 50 percent. And the shift to mobile editing has lagged even more." In 2014 *The New York Times* reported that Möller has assigned "a team of 10 software developers focused on mobile", out of a total of approximately 200 employees working at the Wikimedia Foundation. One principal concern cited by *The New York Times* for the "worry" is for Wikipedia to effectively address attrition issues with the number of editors which the online encyclopedia attracts to edit and maintain its content in a mobile access environment.[^cite_note-small_screen-77] By 2023, the Wikimedia Foundation's staff had grown to over 700 employees.[^cite_note-WF10.23.23-6]

Access to Wikipedia from mobile phones was possible as early as 2004, through the [Wireless Application Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wireless_Application_Protocol) (WAP), via the [Wapedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wapedia) service.[^cite_note-\:16-388] In June 2007, Wikipedia launched en.mobile.wikipedia.org, an official website for wireless devices. In 2009, a newer mobile service was officially released, located at en.m.wikipedia.org, which caters to more advanced mobile devices such as the [iPhone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone), [Android](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Android_\(operating_system\))-based devices, or [WebOS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WebOS)-based devices.[^cite_note-WM_mobile_added_1-406] Several other methods of mobile access to Wikipedia have emerged since. Many devices and applications optimize or enhance the display of Wikipedia content for mobile devices, while some also incorporate additional features such as use of Wikipedia [metadata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metadata) like [geoinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographic_data_and_information).[^cite_note-androgeoid.com_LPOI_WP_1-407][^cite_note-ilounge_iphone_gems_WP-408]

The Android app for Wikipedia was released in January 2012, to over 500,000 installs and generally positive reviews, scoring over four of a possible five in a poll of approximately 200,000 users downloading from Google.[^cite_note-409][^cite_note-410] The version for iOS was released on April 3, 2013, to similar reviews.[^cite_note-411] [Wikipedia Zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia_Zero) was an initiative of the Wikimedia Foundation to expand the reach of the encyclopedia to the developing countries by partnering with mobile operators to allow free access.[^cite_note-\:17-412][^cite_note-413] It was discontinued in February 2018 due to lack of participation from mobile operators.[^cite_note-\:17-412]

[Andrew Lih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Lih) and [Andrew Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Brown_\(writer\)) both maintain that editing Wikipedia with [smartphones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smartphone) is difficult, and this discourages new potential contributors.[^cite_note-Lih-2015-414][^cite_note-Brown-2015-415] Lih states that the number of Wikipedia editors has been declining after several years,[^cite_note-Lih-2015-414] and Tom Simonite of *[MIT Technology Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MIT_Technology_Review)* claims the bureaucratic structure and rules are a factor in this. Simonite alleges some [Wikipedians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedian) use the labyrinthine rules and guidelines to dominate others and those editors have a vested interest in keeping the status quo.[^cite_note-Simonite-2013-54] Lih alleges there is a serious disagreement among existing contributors on how to resolve this. Lih fears for Wikipedia's long-term future while Brown fears problems with Wikipedia will remain, and rival encyclopedias will not replace it.[^cite_note-Lih-2015-414][^cite_note-Brown-2015-415]

### Chinese access

Access to Wikipedia has been [blocked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_censorship_in_China) in [mainland China](55079.md) since May 2015.[^cite_note-Skipper-2015-15][^cite_note-416][^cite_note-417] This was done after Wikipedia started to use [HTTPS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HTTPS) encryption, which made selective censorship more difficult.[^cite_note-418]

## Cultural influence

### Trusted source to combat fake news

In 2017–18, after a barrage of false news reports, both Facebook and YouTube announced they would rely on Wikipedia to help their users evaluate reports and reject false news.[^cite_note-419][^cite_note-Cohen-2018-420] [Noam Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noam_Cohen), writing in *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* states, "YouTube's reliance on Wikipedia to set the record straight builds on the thinking of another fact-challenged platform, the Facebook social network, which announced last year that Wikipedia would help its users root out '[fake news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news)'."[^cite_note-Cohen-2018-420][^cite_note-421]

### Readership

In February 2014, *The New York Times* reported that Wikipedia was ranked fifth globally among all websites, stating "With 18 billion page views and nearly 500 million unique visitors a month, ... Wikipedia trails just Yahoo, Facebook, Microsoft, and Google, the largest with 1.2 billion unique visitors."[^cite_note-small_screen-77] However, its ranking dropped to 13th globally by June 2020 due mostly to a rise in popularity of Chinese websites for online shopping.[^cite_note-Alexa-60] The website has since recovered its ranking as of April 2022.[^cite_note-Alexa-60]

In addition to [logistic growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logistic_function) in the number of its articles,[^cite_note-modelling-422] Wikipedia has steadily gained status as a general reference website since its inception in 2001.[^cite_note-comscore-423] The number of readers of Wikipedia worldwide reached 365 million at the end of 2009.[^cite_note-365M-424] The [Pew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) Internet and American Life project found that one third of US Internet users consulted Wikipedia.[^cite_note-Wikipedia_users-425] In 2011, *[Business Insider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_Insider)* gave Wikipedia a valuation of \$4 billion if it ran advertisements.[^cite_note-426]

According to "Wikipedia Readership Survey 2011", the average age of Wikipedia readers is 36, with a rough parity between genders. Almost half of Wikipedia readers visit the site more than five times a month, and a similar number of readers specifically look for Wikipedia in search engine results. About 47 percent of Wikipedia readers do not realize that Wikipedia is a non-profit organization.[^cite_note-427] As of February 2023, Wikipedia attracts around 2 billion unique devices monthly, with the English Wikipedia receiving 10 billion [pageviews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Pageview_statistics) each month.[^cite_note-Wikimedia_Stats-9]

#### COVID-19 pandemic

Main article: [Wikipedia and the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia_and_the_COVID-19_pandemic)

During the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), Wikipedia's coverage of the pandemic and fight against misinformation received international media attention, and brought an increase in Wikipedia readership overall.[^cite_note-428][^cite_note-Cohen-2020-429][^cite_note-430][^cite_note-431] Noam Cohen wrote in *[Wired](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wired_\(magazine\))* that Wikipedia's effort to combat [misinformation related to the pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_misinformation) was different from other major websites, opining, "Unless Twitter, Facebook and [the others](5897742.md) can learn to address misinformation more effectively, Wikipedia will remain the last best place on the Internet."[^cite_note-Cohen-2020-429] In October 2020, the [World Health Organization](33583.md) announced they were freely licensing its [infographics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infographic) and other materials on Wikimedia projects.[^cite_note-432] There were nearly 7,000 COVID-19 related Wikipedia articles across 188 different Wikipedias, as of November 2021.[^cite_note-433][^cite_note-434]

### Cultural significance

Main article: [Wikipedia in culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia_in_culture)



*[Wikipedia Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia_Monument)* in [Słubice](656704.md), Poland, by [Mihran Hakobyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mihran_Hakobyan) (2014)

Wikipedia's content has also been used in academic studies, books, conferences, and court cases.[^cite_note-Wikipedia_in_media-435][^cite_note-Bourgeois-436][^cite_note-ssrn.com_Wikipedian_Justice_1-437] The [Parliament of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada)'s website refers to Wikipedia's article on [same-sex marriage](92656.md) in the "related links" section of its "further reading" list for the *[Civil Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Marriage_Act)*.[^cite_note-parl.gc.ca_same-sex_marriage-438] The encyclopedia's assertions are increasingly used as a source by organizations such as the US federal courts and the [World Intellectual Property Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Intellectual_Property_Organization)[^cite_note-WP_court_source-439]—though mainly for supporting information rather than information decisive to a case.[^cite_note-Courts_turn_to_Wikipedia-440] Content appearing on Wikipedia has also been cited as a source and referenced in some [US intelligence agency](501244.md) reports.[^cite_note-US_Intelligence-441] In December 2008, the scientific journal *[RNA Biology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNA_Biology)* launched a new section for descriptions of families of RNA molecules and requires authors who contribute to the section to also submit a draft article on the [RNA family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rfam) for publication in Wikipedia.[^cite_note-Declan-442] Wikipedia has also been used as a source in journalism,[^cite_note-ajr.org_WP_in_the_newsroom-443][^cite_note-twsY23-444] often without attribution, and several reporters have been dismissed for [plagiarizing from Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plagiarism_from_Wikipedia).[^cite_note-shizuoka_plagiarized_WP_1-445][^cite_note-446][^cite_note-starbulletin.com_Inquiry_prompts_dismissal-447][^cite_note-448]

In 2006, *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))* magazine recognized Wikipedia's participation (along with YouTube, [Reddit](3829005.md), [MySpace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MySpace), and Facebook) in the rapid growth of online collaboration and interaction by millions of people worldwide.[^cite_note-Time2006-449] On September 16, 2007, *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* reported that Wikipedia had become a focal point in the [2008 US election campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election), saying: "Type a candidate's name into Google, and among the first results is a Wikipedia page, making those entries arguably as important as any ad in defining a candidate. Already, the presidential entries are being edited, dissected and debated countless times each day."[^cite_note-WP.com_WP_election_usage-450] An October 2007 [Reuters](18998750.md) article, titled "Wikipedia page the latest status symbol", reported the recent phenomenon of how having a Wikipedia article vindicates one's notability.[^cite_note-451]

One of the first times Wikipedia was involved in a governmental affair was on September 28, 2007, when [Italian politician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Italy) [Franco Grillini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco_Grillini) raised a parliamentary question with the minister of cultural resources and activities about the necessity of [freedom of panorama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_panorama). He said that the lack of such freedom forced Wikipedia, "the seventh most consulted website", to forbid all images of modern Italian buildings and art, and claimed this was hugely damaging to tourist revenues.[^cite_note-452]

Wikipedia, an introduction – [Erasmus Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erasmus_Prize) 2015



[Jimmy Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Wales) accepts the 2008 [Quadriga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadriga_\(award\)) *A Mission of Enlightenment* award on behalf of Wikipedia.

A working group led by [Peter Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Stone_\(professor\)) (formed as a part of the [Stanford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanford)-based project [One Hundred Year Study on Artificial Intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Hundred_Year_Study_on_Artificial_Intelligence)) in its report called Wikipedia "the best-known example of crowdsourcing ... that far exceeds traditionally-compiled information sources, such as encyclopedias and dictionaries, in scale and depth".[^cite_note-453][^cite_note-NYT-20230718-454]

In a 2017 opinion piece for *[Wired](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wired_\(magazine\))*, [Hossein Derakhshan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hossein_Derakhshan) describes Wikipedia as "one of the last remaining pillars of the [open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Openness) and [decentralized web](49139.md#Centralization_and_redecentralization_of_the_Internet)" and contrasted its existence as a text-based source of knowledge with social media and [social networking services](2041117.md), the latter having "since colonized the web for television's values". For Derakhshan, Wikipedia's goal as an encyclopedia represents the [Age of Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) tradition of [rationality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationality) triumphing over emotions, a trend which he considers "endangered" due to the "gradual shift from a [typographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typography) culture to a photographic one, which in turn mean\[s] a shift from rationality to emotions, exposition to entertainment". Rather than "*[sapere aude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sapere_aude)*" ([lit.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literal_translation) 'dare to know'), social networks have led to a culture of "dare not to care to know". This is while Wikipedia faces "a more concerning problem" than funding, namely "a flattening growth rate in the number of contributors to the website". Consequently, the challenge for Wikipedia and those who use it is to "save Wikipedia and its promise of a free and open collection of all human knowledge amid the conquest of new and old television—how to collect and preserve knowledge when nobody cares to know."[^cite_note-455]

#### Awards



Wikipedia team visiting the Parliament of Asturias



Wikipedians meeting after the 2015 Asturias awards ceremony

Wikipedia has won many awards, receiving its first two major awards in May 2004.[^cite_note-456] The first was a Golden Nica for Digital Communities of the annual [Prix Ars Electronica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prix_Ars_Electronica) contest; this came with a €10,000 (£6,588; \$12,700) grant and an invitation to present at the PAE Cyberarts Festival in Austria later that year. The second was a Judges' [Webby Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Webby_Award) for the "community" category.[^cite_note-webbyawards_WP_awards_1-457]

In September 2008, Wikipedia received [Quadriga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadriga_\(award\)) *A Mission of Enlightenment* award of Werkstatt Deutschland along with [Boris Tadić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Tadi%C4%87), [Eckart Höfling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eckart_H%C3%B6fling), and [Peter Gabriel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Gabriel). The award was presented to Wales by [David Weinberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Weinberger).[^cite_note-loomarea.com_WP_award_1-458]

In 2015, Wikipedia was awarded both the annual [Erasmus Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erasmus_Prize), which recognizes exceptional contributions to culture, society or social sciences,[^cite_note-EP2015-459] and the Spanish [Princess of Asturias Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princess_of_Asturias_Award) on International Cooperation.[^cite_note-460] Speaking at the Asturian Parliament in Oviedo, the city that hosts the awards ceremony, [Jimmy Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Wales) praised the work of the [Asturian Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asturian_Wikipedia) users.[^cite_note-461]

#### Satire

See also: [Category\:Parodies of Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Parodies_of_Wikipedia)

Comedian [Stephen Colbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Colbert) has parodied or referenced Wikipedia on numerous episodes of his show *[The Colbert Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Colbert_Report)* and coined the related term *[wikiality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikiality)*, meaning "together we can create a reality that we all agree on—the reality we just agreed on".[^cite_note-wikiality-254] Another example can be found in "Wikipedia Celebrates 750 Years of American Independence", a July 2006 front-page article in *[The Onion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Onion)*,[^cite_note-onion_WP_750_years_1-462] as well as the 2010 *The Onion* article "'L.A. Law' Wikipedia Page Viewed 874 Times Today".[^cite_note-463]

In an April 2007 episode of the American television comedy *[The Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Office_\(American_TV_series\))*, office manager ([Michael Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Scott_\(The_Office\))) is shown relying on a hypothetical Wikipedia article for information on [negotiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negotiation) tactics to assist him in negotiating lesser pay for an employee.[^cite_note-464] Viewers of the show tried to add the episode's mention of the page as a section of the actual Wikipedia article on negotiation, but this effort was prevented by other users on the article's talk page.[^cite_note-465]

"[My Number One Doctor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/My_Number_One_Doctor)", a 2007 episode of the television show *[Scrubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scrubs_\(2001_TV_series\))*, played on the perception that Wikipedia is an unreliable reference tool with a scene in which [Perry Cox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perry_Cox) reacts to a patient who says that a Wikipedia article indicates that the [raw food diet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raw_food_diet) reverses the effects of [bone cancer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bone_cancer) by retorting that the same editor who wrote that article also wrote the [*Battlestar Galactica* episode guide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Battlestar_Galactica_\(2004_TV_series\)_episodes).[^cite_note-Bakken_one_doctor_1-466]

In 2008, the comedy website *[CollegeHumor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CollegeHumor)* produced a video sketch named "Professor Wikipedia", in which the fictitious Professor Wikipedia instructs a class with a medley of unverifiable and occasionally absurd statements.[^cite_note-467][^cite_note-468] The *[Dilbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilbert)* comic strip from May 8, 2009, features a character supporting an improbable claim by saying, "Give me ten minutes and then check Wikipedia."[^cite_note-469] In July 2009, [BBC Radio 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Radio_4) broadcast a comedy series called *[Bigipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bigipedia)*, which was set on a website which was a parody of Wikipedia.[^cite_note-470] Some of the sketches were directly inspired by Wikipedia and its articles.[^cite_note-471]

On August 23, 2013, the *[New Yorker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Yorker)* website published a cartoon with this caption: "Dammit, Manning, have you considered the pronoun war that this is going to start on your Wikipedia page?"[^cite_note-472] The cartoon referred to [Chelsea Elizabeth Manning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chelsea_Manning) (born Bradley Edward Manning), an American activist, politician, and former United States Army soldier who had recently [come out](71537.md) as a [trans woman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans_woman).[^cite_note-473]

In June 2024, *[Nature](43427.md)* published a fictional Wikipedia [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk_page) under the title "Plastic-eating fungus caused doomsday" by Emma Burnett. The talk page concerned a fictional article describing the unintended consequences of the release of a plastic-eating fungus to clean up an oil spill. The article contained talk page topics found on Wikipedia, like discussions of changes in the article's assessment.[^cite_note-474]

### Publishing



A group of Wikimedians of the [Wikimedia DC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikimedia_DC) chapter at the 2013 DC Wikimedia annual meeting standing in front of the *Encyclopædia Britannica* *(back left)* at the US National Archives

The most obvious economic effect of Wikipedia has been the death of commercial encyclopedias, especially printed versions like *[Encyclopædia Britannica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclop%C3%A6dia_Britannica)*, which were unable to compete with a free alternative.[^cite_note-475][^cite_note-476][^cite_note-FT_effect_on_traditional_media-477] [Nicholas Carr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_G._Carr)'s 2005 essay "The amorality of [Web 2.0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Web_2.0)" criticizes websites with [user-generated content](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/User-generated_content) (like Wikipedia) for possibly leading to professional (and, in his view, superior) content producers' going out of business, because "free trumps quality all the time". Carr wrote, "Implicit in the ecstatic visions of Web 2.0 is the [hegemony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hegemony) of the amateur. I for one can't imagine anything more frightening."[^cite_note-RType_WP_traditional_media_effect_1-478] Others dispute the notion that Wikipedia, or similar efforts, will entirely displace traditional publications. [Chris Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Anderson_\(writer\)), the former editor-in-chief of *[Wired](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wired_\(magazine\))*, wrote in *[Nature](43427.md)* that the "[wisdom of crowds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisdom_of_the_crowd)" approach of Wikipedia will not displace top [scientific journals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_journal) with rigorous [peer review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peer_review) processes.[^cite_note-nature.com_crowds_wisdom-479]

Wikipedia's influence on the biography publishing business has been a concern for some. Book publishing data tracker Nielsen [BookScan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BookScan) stated in 2013 that biography sales were dropping "far more sharply".[^cite_note-Flood-2013-480] [Kathryn Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathryn_Hughes), professor of [life writing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_writing) at the [University of East Anglia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_East_Anglia) and author of two biographies wrote, "The worry is that, if you can get all that information from Wikipedia, what's left for biography?"[^cite_note-Flood-2013-480]

### Research use

Wikipedia has been widely used as a [corpus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Text_corpus) for linguistic research in [computational linguistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computational_linguistics), [information retrieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_retrieval) and [natural language processing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_language_processing).[^cite_note-481][^cite_note-482] In particular, it commonly serves as a target knowledge base for the [entity linking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entity_linking) problem, which is then called "wikification",[^cite_note-wikify-483] and to the related problem of [word-sense disambiguation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Word-sense_disambiguation).[^cite_note-milne_witten_WP_usage_1-484] Methods similar to wikification can in turn be used to find "missing" links in Wikipedia.[^cite_note-discovering_missing_WP_links_1-485]

In 2015, French researchers José Lages of the [University of Franche-Comté](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Franche-Comt%C3%A9) in [Besançon](86918.md) and Dima Shepelyansky of [Paul Sabatier University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Sabatier_University) in [Toulouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toulouse) published a global university ranking based on Wikipedia scholarly citations.[^cite_note-mitmining-486][^cite_note-harvardisonlymarmow-487][^cite_note-wikipediarankingtimesworldunifranche-488] They used [PageRank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PageRank), [CheiRank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CheiRank) and similar algorithms "followed by the number of appearances in the 24 different language editions of Wikipedia (descending order) and the century in which they were founded (ascending order)".[^cite_note-wikipediarankingtimesworldunifranche-488][^cite_note-489] The study was updated in 2019.[^cite_note-490]

In December 2015, [John Julius Norwich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Julius_Norwich) stated, in a letter published in *[The Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times)* newspaper, that as a historian he resorted to Wikipedia "at least a dozen times a day", and had "never caught it out". He described it as "a work of reference as useful as any in existence", with so wide a range that it is almost impossible to find a person, place, or thing that it has left uncovered and that he could never have written his last two books without it.[^cite_note-491]

A 2017 [MIT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MIT) study suggests that words used in Wikipedia articles end up in scientific publications.[^cite_note-492] Studies related to Wikipedia have been using [machine learning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machine_learning) and [artificial intelligence](1164.md)[^cite_note-NYT-20230718-454] to support various operations. One of the most important areas is the automatic detection of vandalism[^cite_note-493][^cite_note-494] and [data quality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_quality) assessment in Wikipedia.[^cite_note-495][^cite_note-496]

## Related projects

Several interactive multimedia encyclopedias incorporating entries written by the public existed long before Wikipedia was founded. The first of these was the 1986 [BBC Domesday Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Domesday_Project), which included text (entered on [BBC Micro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Micro) computers) and photographs from more than a million contributors in the UK, and covered the geography, art, and culture of the UK. This was the first interactive multimedia encyclopedia (and was also the first major multimedia document connected through internal links), with the majority of articles being accessible through an interactive map of the UK. The user interface and part of the content of the Domesday Project were emulated on a website until 2008.[^cite_note-Domesday_Project-497]

Several free-content, collaborative encyclopedias were created around the same period as Wikipedia (e.g. [Everything2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everything2)),[^cite_note-498] with many later being merged into the project (e.g. [GNE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GNE_\(encyclopedia\))).[^cite_note-499] One of the most successful early online encyclopedias incorporating entries by the public was [h2g2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H2g2), which was created by [Douglas Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Adams) in 1999. The h2g2 encyclopedia is relatively lighthearted, focusing on articles which are both witty and informative.[^cite_note-500]

Subsequent collaborative [knowledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge_base) websites have drawn inspiration from Wikipedia. Others use more traditional [peer review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peer_review), such as *[Encyclopedia of Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclopedia_of_Life)* and the online wiki encyclopedias *[Scholarpedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholarpedia)* and [Citizendium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizendium).[^cite_note-501][^cite_note-502] The latter was started by Sanger in an attempt to create a reliable alternative to Wikipedia.[^cite_note-Orlowski18-503][^cite_note-JayLyman-504]

  Jimmy Wales, founder of Wikipedia, discusses the site, how it's treated by governments, and how it's fueled by its users.

- [The Great Book of Knowledge, Part 1: A Wiki is a Kind of Bus](https://www.cbc.ca/ideas/episodes/2014/01/15/the-great-book-of-knowledge-part-1/), *[Ideas, with Paul Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideas_\(radio_show\))*, [CBC Radio One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBC_Radio_One), originally broadcast January 15, 2014. The webpage includes a link to the archived audio program (also [found here](https://www.cbc.ca/ideas/popupaudio.html?clipIds=2430203709)). The radio documentary discusses Wikipedia's history, development, and its place within the broader scope of the trend to democratized knowledge. It also includes interviews with several key Wikipedia staff and contributors, including [Kat Walsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kat_Walsh) and [Sue Gardner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_Gardner) (audio, 53\:58, Flash required).

[^cite_note-1]: &#x20;There are 348 active editions of Wikipedia, but that does not translate into 348 unique languages. For example, there is an edition in English and an edition in Simple English, different editions of Belarusan (using the [Narkamaŭka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narkama%C5%ADka) and [Taraškievica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tara%C5%A1kievica) orthographies), different editions of Norwegian (using the [Nynorsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nynorsk) and [Bokmål](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bokm%C3%A5l) orthographies), etc.\
    Additionally, there are 16 closed editions of Wikipedia, in languages for which there are not enough speakers for sustainment (for example, [Marshallese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshallese_language) and [Akan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akan_language)). Four editions were outright deleted (examples include the [Moldovian Wikipedia](https://meta.wikimedia.org/wiki/Proposals%20for%20closing%20projects/Deletion%20of%20Moldovan%20Wikipedia%202) and the [Siberian Wikipedia](https://meta.wikimedia.org/wiki/Proposals%20for%20closing%20projects/Deletion%20of%20Siberian%20Wikipedia))

[^cite_note-2]: &#x20;For some nearly extinct languages, such as the [Manchu language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchu_language), no Wikipedia editions have ever been created.

[^cite_note-3]: &#x20;Registration is required for certain tasks, such as editing [protected pages](#Restrictions), creating pages on the English Wikipedia, and uploading files.

[^cite_note-4]: &#x20;Most text is also dual-licensed under [GFDL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GNU_Free_Documentation_License); media licensing varies.

[Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20081218013300/http://www.cnn.com/2003/TECH/internet/08/03/wikipedia/index.html) from the original on December 18, 2008. Retrieved December 26, 2008.

[^cite_note-171]: &#x20;[Sanger, Larry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Sanger) (December 31, 2004). ["Why Wikipedia Must Jettison Its Anti-Elitism"](https://web.archive.org/web/20211101011352/http://www.kuro5hin.org/story/2004/12/30/142458/25). *[Kuro5hin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuro5hin), Op–Ed*. Archived from [the original](https://www.kuro5hin.org/story/2004/12/30/142458/25) on November 1, 2021. Retrieved March 26, 2021. There is a certain mindset associated with unmoderated Usenet groups \[...] that infects the collectively-managed Wikipedia project: if you react strongly to trolling, that reflects poorly on you, not (necessarily) on the troll. If you \[...] demand that something be done about constant disruption by trollish behavior, the other listmembers will cry "censorship", attack you, and even come to the defense of the troll. \[...] The root problem: anti-elitism, or lack of respect for expertise. There is a deeper problem \[...] which explains both of the above-elaborated problems. Namely, as a community, Wikipedia lacks the habit or tradition of respect for expertise. As a community, far from being elitist, it is anti-elitist (which, in this context, means that expertise is not accorded any special respect, and snubs and disrespect of expertise are tolerated). This is one of my failures: a policy that I attempted to institute in Wikipedia's first year, but for which I did not muster adequate support, was the policy of respecting and deferring politely to experts. (Those who were there will, I hope, remember that I tried very hard.)
